indicating that for a given system
\sigma
, under layout
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
,
the system evolves from state
S
with directive
d\in\mathsf{Dir}
to
S^{\prime}
in one step,
producing the side-channel observation
o
.
The state of a system
S
is now modeled as a stack of backtrackable configurations.
Specifically, configurations follow the following BNF:

ϕ
,
χ

(F,
μ
m,b_​
ms
)

\mid

(
err
, b_​
ms
)

\mid

unsafe

In a configuration
(F,{{\mu}m},b_{\!\mathit{ms}})
,

F
is a call-stack as in
Section

3
,
{{\mu}m}
is a memory equipped with a
write buffer

{\mu}
, and
b_{\!\mathit{ms}}
the
mis-speculation
flag.
Buffered memories
{{\mu}m}
permit out-of-order, speculative memory operations.
Specifically, writing a value
v
at address
p
results in a delayed write
[p\mapsto v]{{\mu}m}
, and
{({{\mu}m})^{k}}(p)
yields the

k
th-last buffered entry
v
at address
p
, together with a boolean flag
f
that it
\bot
if and only if
v
is the most recent one associated to address
p
.
This operation is formally described by the following function:

\displaystyle{({[]m})^{k}}(p)

\displaystyle\triangleq m(a),\bot

\displaystyle{({{[p\mapsto n]}\mathrel{:}{\mu}m})^{0}}(p)

\displaystyle\triangleq n,\bot

\displaystyle{({{[p\mapsto n]}\mathrel{:}{\mu}m})^{i+1}}(p)

\displaystyle\triangleq n^{\prime},\top

\displaystyle\text{if }{({{\mu}m})^{i}}(p)=n^{\prime},b

\displaystyle{({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto n]}\mathrel{:}{\mu}m})^{i}}(p)

\displaystyle\triangleq{({{\mu}m})^{i}}(p)

\displaystyle\text{if }p\neq p^{\prime}.

In a configuration, the mis-speculation flag
b_{\!\mathit{ms}}
records whether a past
step of computation led to a mis-speculation.
It is employed when backtracking from a speculative state.
As errors are recoverable under mis-speculation,
error configurations
\mathsf{err}
carry also a mis-speculation flag. Finally,
as in
Section

3
,
\mathsf{unsafe}
indicates
a safety violation.

Some illustrative rules of the semantics are given in
Figure

5
,
the complete set of rules is relegated to the appendix, see
Figures

7
and
8
.The rules for a load instruction are very similar to the ones we give in

Section

3
, but attackers can take advantage of
the store-to-load dependency speculation by issuing a
\mathsf{ld}_{\ell}\,i

directive. When this happens, the
i
-th most recent value
associated to the address
p=\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}^{\mathsf{Addr}}

is retrieved from the buffered memory, see rule
.
This value may not correspond to that of the most recent
store to the address
p
, and this is signaled by the flag
f

that is returned after the buffer lookup. If
f=\top
,
this operation may be engaging mis-speculation and, for this reason,
the semantics keeps track of the current configuration in the stack.
A successful load produces the observation
{\mathsf{mem}\,p}

that leaks the address to the attacker.
The rules for erroneous and unsafe loads are

and

and they are analogous to their non-speculative counterparts.
In our semantics, every command supports the
\mathsf{st}
directive,
which evaluates the configuration without speculating. For instance,
the
rule evaluates the
x\ \mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\text{{*}}{\mathtt{E}}
command
by fetching the most recent value from the write buffer, instead of an
arbitrary one.

Even branch instructions can be executed speculatively by issuing
the directive
\mathsf{br}\,d
by means of the rule
.
This causes the evaluation to continue as if the guard resolved to

d
. This operation leaks which branch is being executed
by means of the observation
{\mathsf{br}\,d}
. Even in this case,
the rule may be mis-speculating; for this reason, the current configuration
is book-kept in the stack and the mis-speculation flag is updated.

When the topmost configuration of a stack carries
the mis-speculation flag
\top
,
the configuration can be is discarded with the
rules
.
If it is
\bot
, the current
state is not mis-speculating, so the whole stack
of book-kept configurations can be discarded with
the rule
.

The
rule commits all the entries in the
write buffer to the memory. Precisely, this operation is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\overline{{[]m}}

\displaystyle\triangleq m

\displaystyle\overline{{{[p\mapsto v]}:{\mu}m}}

\displaystyle\triangleq{\overline{{{\mu}m}}[{p}\leftarrow{v}]},

where, by
{m[{p}\leftarrow{v}]}
, we denote the memory
obtained by updating the value at address
p
with
v
.
In particular, for consistency, a potentially mis-speculative state must
be resolved. This is why this rule requires the mis-speculation flag to
be
\bot
. This means that, if this configuration is reached
when the flag is
\top
, the semantics must backtrack
with the rule
.

6.1.2
Attacker’s Language and Semantics

To give a definition of kernel safety w.r.t. speculative semantics,
we endow an attacker with the ability to engage in speculative executions,
by issuing directives, and by the ability to read side-channel information.
To this end, we extend the instructions from
Section

3

as follows:

Instr
∋
SI

…

spec

on

C

speculation on victim
\mathtt{C}

poison
(d)

speculative poisoning

x

:=

observe
()

side-channel observation

SpAdv
∋
A

ε

\mid

SI
;
A

The instruction
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{spec}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{on}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}\end{array}
is used to execute a victim code
\mathtt{C}
,
w.r.t. the speculative semantics defined just above.
By using the instruction

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{poison}}}({d})
, the attacker is able to to mistrain microarchitectural
predictors and to control the speculative execution.
Issued directives control the evaluation of victim code under speculative semantics.
Dual, the instruction
x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{observe}}}()
is used to extract side-channel info-leaks, collected
during speculative execution of the victim’s code. To model this operation, in the following,
we assume
\mathsf{Obs}\subseteq\mathsf{Val}
.
As an example, the snippet

{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{poison}}}({\mathsf{br}_{\ell}\,\top}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{spec}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{on}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{if}}}_{\ell}\ \mathtt{E}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{then}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(p)\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fi}}}\end{array}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\\
x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{observe}}}()\end{array}}

(†)

forces the mis-speculative execution of
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(p)
, independently of
the value of
\mathtt{E}
. The register
x
will hold the final observation leaked through executing the system call.

The attacker’s semantics is defined in terms of a relation

w\vdash_{\sigma}\phi\mathbin{\hbox to11.53pt{\vbox to11.73pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.33301pt\lower-5.86601pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

In essence, the attacker executes under the standard semantics given in
Section

3
,
the speculative semantics defined above play a role only when execution of the
victim is triggered by the directive
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{spec}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{on}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}\end{array}
.
Consequently, configurations are identical in structure to
the ones underlying the standard semantics, but carry however
additionally stacks
D
and
O
of directives and observations,
in order to model the new constructs.
In addition, hybrid configurations
S\,|\,(F,D,O)
are used
to model the system when executing the victim under speculative semantics.
Here
S
is a stack of speculative configurations concerning the victim,
and
F
the attacker’s call stack
up to the invocation of speculation. Again,
D
gives the
directives (to be processed) and
O
the observations (collected from executing victim’s code).
In summary, configurations are drawn from the following BNF:

ϕ

(F,m,D,O)

\mid

S —  (F,D,O)

\mid

err

\mid

unsafe

Figure

6
shows the evaluation rules for the new constructs.
Rules
and
define the semantics for poisoning
and side-channel observations, by pushing and redacting elements of the corresponding stacks.
Rule
deals with the initialization
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{spec}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{on}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}\end{array}
of speculative execution,
starting from the corresponding initial configuration of the victim
\mathtt{C}
in an empty speculation context.
A frame for the continuation of the attacker
\mathtt{A}
is pushed on the call stack
S
.
This frame is used to resume execution of the attacker, once the victim has been
fully evaluated.
The victim itself is evaluated
via the speculative semantics through rules
–
.
Note how execution of the victim is directed through the directive stack
D
(rule
).
Should the current directive be inapplicable, a non-speculative rewrite step (rule
)
or backtracking (rule
) is performed. Here,
the premise
w\vdash_{\sigma}S{\downarrow}_{d}
signifies that
S
is irreducible
w.r.t. the directive
d
. Likewise,
w\vdash_{\sigma}S{\downarrow}_{D}
means that

S
is irreducible w.r.t. the topmost directive of
D
, or that
D
is empty.
Note also how side-channel leakage, modeled through observations, is
collected in the configuration via these rules.
Upon normal termination, resuming of evaluation of the attacker is
governed by rule
in the case of normal termination.
Finally, rules
and
deal with abnormal termination.

6.2
Speculative Kernel Safety

We are now ready to extend the definition of kernel safety (
Definition

1
)
to the speculative semantics.

Definition 3

(Speculative kernel safety)
.

We say that a system
\sigma=(\tau,\gamma,\xi)
is
speculative kernel safe

if for every unprivileged attacker
\mathtt{A}\in\mathtt{SpAdv}
,
every layout
w
,
and register map
\rho
, we have:

\lnot\left(w\vdash_{\sigma}(\langle\mathtt{A},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau,\epsilon,\epsilon)\mathbin{\hbox to15.13pt{\vbox to11.73pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.33301pt\lower-5.86601pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.74 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.41469pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.88 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.48 0 L 0.28 2.45 L 0 0 L 0.28 -2.45 Z}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.0pt}{-2.533pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.15 -3.5)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathsf{unsafe}\right).

It is important to note that this safety notion captures violations that
occur during transient execution. This is in line with what happens, for instance,
for Spectre and Meltdown
[
34
,
38
]
, both exploiting unsafe
memory access under transient execution in order to
reveal confidential information.

6.3
The Demise of Layout Randomization in the Spectre Era

A direct consequence of
Definition

3
is that every system that is

speculative kernel safe
is also
kernel safe
.
The inverse, of course, does not hold in general. Most importantly,
the probabilistic form of safety provided by layout randomization
in
Section

5
does not scale to this extended threat model.
This happens because
Definition

1
does
not take side-channel leakage into account. As a simple example, gadgets like

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{if}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}=p\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{then}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{else}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{D}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fi}}},

can be exploited by an attacker to infer information about the address of a kernel-space
procedure
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}
, through side-channel leakage distinguishing the execution of
\mathtt{C}
and
\mathtt{D}
.
In our model, this is reflected as executing this instruction allows the attacker to observe
{\mathsf{br}\,b}
, with
b
being true precisely when
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}
resides at address
p
.
Secondly, speculative execution undermines a fundamental premise crucially leveraged in
Theorem

1
and, more widely, in the majority of studies demonstrating the efficacy of layout randomization as a defense against attacks (e.g.,
[
2
,
1
,
4
]
): the notion that an unsuccessful memory probe leads to abnormal termination, thus thwarting the attack.
Indeed, within transient executions, memory access violations are recoverable.

This happens, for instance, if the system call
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}

of (
†
‣
6.1.2
) tries to load the content of the
address
p
form the memory to a register.
If
p
is not allocated, the system performs a
memory access violation under transient execution,
that does not terminate the execution. Conversely, if
p
is allocated,
its content is loaded into the cache, producing the observation
{\mathsf{mem}\,p}

before the execution of the branch and the system call are backtracked.
By reading side-channel observations, the attacker can thus
distinguish allocated kernel-addresses from those
that are not allocated.
This last example, in particular, is not at all
fictitious: the BlindSide attack
[
25
]

uses the same idea to break Linux’s KASLR
and locate the position of kernel’s executable code
and data.

6.4
Speculative Layout Non-Interference

As this revised model significantly enhances the attackers’ strength,
we will need to implement more stringent countermeasures in order to restore kernel safety.
To counter side-channel info-leaks, we can impose a form of
side-channel non-interference that is in line with the notion
of
speculative constant-time
from
[
14
]
.

Definition 4

(Speculative layout non-interference)
.

Given
\sigma=(\tau,\gamma,\xi)
,
a system call
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}
is
speculative layout non-interfering
, if,

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,{{\mu}(w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})},b_{\!\mathit{ms}})\mathbin{\hbox to18.51pt{\vbox to21.3pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70988pt\lower-10.64934pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.74 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.41469pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.88 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.48 0 L 0.28 2.45 L 0 0 L 0.28 -2.45 Z}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.37688pt}{-7.31633pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -0.52 -10.12)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.14888pt}{2.533pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -0.21 3.5)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{10.20001pt}{-3.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 14.11 -4.84)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}S_{1}

implies

w_{2}\vdash_{\sigma}(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,{{\mu}(w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})},b_{\!\mathit{ms}})\mathbin{\hbox to18.51pt{\vbox to21.3pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70988pt\lower-10.64934pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.41469pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.88 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.48 0 L 0.28 2.45 L 0 0 L 0.28 -2.45 Z}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.37688pt}{-7.31633pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -0.52 -10.12)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.14888pt}{2.533pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -0.21 3.5)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{10.20001pt}{-3.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 14.11 -4.84)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}S_{2},

for all layouts
w_{1},w_{2}
, configurations over stores
\tau^{\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau
coinciding on procedures (
\tau^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}\in\mathsf{FunId}
), directives
D
, observations
O
and register map
\rho
.

Speculative layout non-interference effectively prevents side-channel-related attacks, even during transient executions. Importantly, it ensures the non-leakage of layout information throughout the side-channels by requiring the identity of the two sequences of observations produced by the two reductions. This, however, implies severe restrictions on memory interactions — effectively prohibiting the use of random memory layouts! Unsurprisingly, this form of non-interference directly establishes kernel safety of system calls:

Lemma 3
.

For every system
\sigma=(\tau,\gamma,\xi)
, if

\kappa_{\mathtt{k}}\geq\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}\in\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{k}}}\mathsf{size}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}})+2\cdot\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}\in\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{k}}}\mathsf{size}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}),

and if
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}
is speculative layout non-interfering, then

\lnot\left(w\vdash_{\sigma}(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,{{\mu}(w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})},b_{\!\mathit{ms}})\mathbin{\hbox to18.51pt{\vbox to21.3pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70988pt\lower-10.64934pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.74 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.41469pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.88 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.48 0 L 0.28 2.45 L 0 0 L 0.28 -2.45 Z}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.37688pt}{-7.31633pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -0.52 -10.12)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.14888pt}{2.533pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -0.21 3.5)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{10.20001pt}{-3.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 14.11 -4.84)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathsf{unsafe}\right)

for all layouts
w
and initial configurations over stores
\tau^{\prime}
coinciding with
\tau
on
\mathsf{FunId}
.

The proof of this lemma is in
Section

A.2.4
.

Intuitively, this statement holds because if an invocation of a system call
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}

performs an unsafe memory access when executing under a layout
w
,
the address
p
of the accessed resource is leaked;
but the same address cannot leak if the resource is moved to another location —
this is why we impose the condition on the size of the memory.
Thus, if a system call is speculative layout non-interfering,
it cannot be speculative non-interferent because
different memory layouts produce different observations.

In general, it is not always the case that a non-interference
property has as consequence memory safety.
For instance, being
non-interferent
with respect to a set
of secrets does not prevent a victim program from breaking
memory safety. In our case, this property holds because the layouts
are not only used as the inputs for a computation,
but they also determine
where
objects are placed in memory.

Theorem 2
.

Under the assumption
\kappa_{\mathtt{k}}\geq\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}\in\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{k}}}\mathsf{size}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}})+2\cdot\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}\in\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{k}}}\mathsf{size}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}})
,
if a system
\sigma
is speculative layout non-interfering, then it is
speculative kernel safe
.

This result is demonstrated in
Section

A.2.4
.

Observe that the safety guarantee
provided by
speculative layout non-interference
is not probabilistic.
Although its effectiveness, layout randomization is unlikely to be restored
at the software level without imposing
speculative layout non-interference
,
in presence of this assumption, layout randomization is a redundant protection measure.
Also notice that

speculative layout non-interference
is not a necessary condition for

speculative kernel safety
. For instance, we can take in consideration
the following system:

⬇

void

f
()

{

skip
;

};

void

s
()

{

f
();

};

This system does not enjoy
speculative non-interference
, because by executing

s
, the address of
f
leaks, and this address changes under different layouts.
However, this system is speculatively safe if we assume that
f
belongs
to the capabilities of
s
.

7
Enforcement of Speculative Kernel Safety

Although by requiring
speculative layout non-interference
,
we would be able to restore
speculative kernel safety
, this would
impose important limitations on the system. For this reason,
we believe it is worth investigating whether
speculative kernel safety

can be enforced without imposing
speculative layout non-interference
.

Nevertheless, directly enforcing
speculative kernel safety

is non-trivial, because it requires the developers to constantly
take in account a large variety of microarchitectural behaviors that
their system may run into. On the other hand, in the last decades, plenty of effort
has been put in developing safe code in the classic model,
[
46
,
49
,
51
,
59
,
23
,
37
]
.
So, our last question is to determine to which extent we can
establish a link between kernel safety and
speculative

kernel safety.
Following other works in this direction
[
17
,
58
]
,
our main idea is to nullify the gap between
kernel safety

and
speculative kernel
safety, by making the latter
property a consequence of the former.

This can be achieved by finding a transformation

\zeta
that turns
any
kernel safe
system
\sigma
into another system

\zeta(\sigma)
which is architecturally equivalent to
\sigma

but enjoys
speculatively kernel safety
.
The semantic requirement on the transformation
\zeta

is expressed by
Definition

5
, by which we ask that
no user-space program may show different behaviors by
executing in the two systems.

Definition 5

(Semantics preservation)
.

A system transformation
\zeta
is
user-space semantics preserving

if, for any system
\sigma=(\tau,\gamma,\xi)
,

\mathit{Eval}_{\zeta(\sigma),w}(\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u},\tau^{\prime})\simeq\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}(\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u},\tau)

for every layout
w
, unprivileged command
\mathtt{C}
, and registers
\rho
.
Here,
\tau^{\prime}
is the store underlying
\zeta(\sigma)
.
The equivalence is given by
(v,\tau_{1})\simeq(v,\tau_{2})
if

\tau_{1}=_{\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{u}}}\tau_{2}
, and coincides with equality otherwise.

Notice that in the previous definition we require
\tau_{1}=_{\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{u}}}\tau_{2}
instead
of
\tau_{1}=\tau_{2}
in order to allow the transformation
\zeta

to modify kernel-space procedures.

Thanks to
semantics preservation
, the second requirement on
\zeta

can be fulfilled by asking that the system
\zeta(\sigma)

can violate
speculative kernel safety
only if it violates
kernel safety
, as
captured by
Definition

6
below.

Definition 6
.

We say that
\zeta

imposes speculative kernel safety

if, for every system
\sigma
such that
\zeta(\sigma)=(\tau,\gamma,\xi)
,
every buffer
{\mu}
with
\mathsf{dom}({\mu})\subseteq\underline{w}(\mathsf{ArrId})

and store
\tau^{\prime}=_{\mathsf{Fun}}\tau
,
if

w\vdash_{\zeta(\sigma)}{({(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}})},{{\mu}(w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})},b_{\!\mathit{ms}})}\mathbin{\hbox to18.51pt{\vbox to21.3pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70988pt\lower-10.64934pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.74 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.41469pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.88 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.48 0 L 0.28 2.45 L 0 0 L 0.28 -2.45 Z}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.37688pt}{-7.31633pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -0.52 -10.12)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.14888pt}{2.533pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -0.21 3.5)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{10.20001pt}{-3.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 14.11 -4.84)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathsf{unsafe},

then

w\vdash_{\zeta(\sigma)}{({(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}})},\overline{{{\mu}(w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}})}\to^{*}\mathsf{unsafe}.

Observe that, by means of this property, we can easily
show that if a system
\zeta(\sigma)
is
kernel safe
, then
it is also
speculative kernel safe
. Finally,
by combining
Definition

6
and
Definition

5
,
we obtain the following conclusion:

Proposition 1
.

If a system
\sigma
is
kernel-safe
,
and the transformation
\zeta
(i)
imposes speculative kernel
safety
and (ii) is
user-space semantics preserving
, then
(i)
\zeta(\sigma)
is
speculative kernel safe
,
and (ii)
\zeta(\sigma)
is semantically equivalent to
\sigma
.

This result states that every
kernel safe
system can be transformed
into another system that is equivalent to it
from the user’s perspective and enjoys
stronger security guarantees.
Notice that
kernel safety
cannot be provided
solely by the adoption of layout randomization:
by
Theorem

1
, we know that layout randomization
provides
kernel safety

only modulo a small probability of failure.

Just as an example, we observe that a simple transformation that satisfies
the requirements of
Proposition

1

can be implemented by placing a
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fence}}}
instruction before

all
the potentially unsafe operations. This
instrumentation stops any ongoing speculation before
executing potentially unsafe operations, and prevents their transient
execution, yet leaving the program’s semantics
unaltered at the architectural level.
This
fencing
transformation is
expressed by
\eta:\mathsf{Instr}\to\mathtt{C}

on the level of instructions where, in particular:

\displaystyle\eta({\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}})

\displaystyle\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fence}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}

\displaystyle\eta(x\ \mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\text{{*}}{\mathtt{E}})

\displaystyle\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fence}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}x\ \mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\text{{*}}{\mathtt{E}}

\displaystyle\eta({\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}))

\displaystyle\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fence}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k})

\displaystyle\eta({\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{while}}}\ \mathtt{E}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{do}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{od}}})

\displaystyle\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{while}}}\ \mathtt{E}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{do}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\eta(\mathtt{C})\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{od}}}

\displaystyle\eta({\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{if}}}\ \mathtt{E}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{then}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{else}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{D}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fi}}})

\displaystyle\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{if}}}\ \mathtt{E}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{then}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\eta(\mathtt{C})\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{else}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\eta(\mathtt{D})\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fi}}},

and it is the identity on the remaining instructions. Here,
the transformation is extended homomorphically to a
transformation
\eta:\mathtt{C}\to\mathtt{C}
. It is lifted
to a system
\sigma
by systematically applying it to system calls
and procedures
\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})
in kernel-memory (
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}\in\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{k}}
).

Notice that the transformation
\eta
, does not stop
completely speculation as, for instance,
speculation on conditional instructions is still allowed. This is
not in contrast with
Definition

6
because even in transient
execution, a conditional instruction cannot perform any safety
violation. However, as their branches can contain unsafe operations,
the transformation visits them.

By observing that
\eta
enjoys both the properties in

Definitions

5
and
6
, we can draw the following conclusion:

Theorem 3
.

If a system
\sigma
is
kernel-safe
, then

\eta(\sigma)
is
speculative kernel safe
,
and
\eta(\sigma)
is semantically equivalent to
\sigma
.

This result is proven in
Section

A.2.5
.

In addition to
\eta
, other program
transformations that fit the requirements of

Proposition

1
can be identified:
for instance, the variation of
\eta^{\prime}

that places a single
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fence}}}
instruction before
sequences of loads — or of stores — is a good choice.
Similarly,
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fence}}}
instructions can be omitted before
direct calls. Finally, in presence of an external proof
that shows that a system call
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}
enjoys

speculative kernel safety
, the instrumentation
may decide to leave that system call unchanged, yet
preserving
Definition

6
.

8
Related Work

On Layout Randomization.

The first work that provided a formal account of layout randomization was by
Abadi and Plotkin [2]
, later extended in
[
1
,
4
]
. In these works, the authors show that layout randomization prevents, with high probability, malicious programs from accessing the memory of a victim in an execution context with shared address space. We have already discussed this in the body of the
paper how these results do not
model speculative execution or side-channel observations.

Spatial Memory Safety and Non-Interference

Spatial memory safety is typically defined by associating a software component with a memory area and requiring that, at runtime, it only accesses that area
[
10
,
47
,
45
]
.
Azevedo de Amorim et al. [7]
demonstrated that memory safety can be expressed in terms of non-interference; this property, in turn, stipulates that the final output of a computation is not influenced by secret data that a program must keep confidential
[
24
]
. Both of these properties have been extended to the speculative model. The definition of
speculative memory safety
from
[
10
]
closely aligns with ours, while
speculative non-interference
was initially introduced in the context of the
Spectector
symbolic analyzer
[
28
]
.
Spectector
’s property captures information flows to side-channels that occur with speculative execution but not in sequential execution. In contrast to
Spectector
’s approach, our definition aligns with
speculative constant-time

[
14
]
, as it specifically targets information leaks that occur with speculative semantics.

Formal Analysis of Security Properties of Privileged Execution Environments.

Barthe et al. [9]
deploy a model with side-channel leaks and privileged execution mode, without specualtive execution. In particular, they are interested in studying the preservation of constant-time in virtualization platforms. They also model privilege-raising procedures
hypercalls
, similar to our system calls. They show that if one of the hosts is constant-time then the system enjoys a form of non-interference with respect to that host’s secret memory. For this reason, although the two models are similar, the purposes of
Barthe et al. [9]
and our work are different: in
[
9
]
the victim and the attacker have the same levels of privilege and the role of the hypervisor is to ensure their separation whilst, in our work, the privileged code base is itself the victim.

Attacks to Kernel Layout Randomization

Attacks that aim at leaking information on the kernel’s layout are very popular and can rely on implementation bugs that reveal information the kernel’s layout
[
36
,
41
,
15
]
or on side-channel info-leaks
[
26
,
35
,
40
,
39
,
39
]
. In particular attacks such as EchoLoad, TagBleed and EntryBleed
[
35
,
40
,
12
]
are successful even in presence of state-of-art mitigations such as Intel’s Page Table Isolation (PTI)
[
33
]
. These attacks motivate our decision to take into account side-channel info-leaks. Due to address-space separation between kernel and user space programs, an attacker cannot easily use a pointer to a kernel address to access the victim’s memory. So, in general, if the attacker does not control the value of a pointer that is used by the victim, this kind of leak is not harmful.

The Meltdown attack
[
38
]
uses speculative execution to overcome this limitation on operating systems running on Intel processors that do not adopt KAISER
[
27
]
or PTI
[
33
]
. In particular, the hardware can speculatively access an address before checking its permissions. The attack uses this small time window to access kernel memory content and leak it by using a side-channel info-leak gadget. These attacks can also be used to leak information on the layout: by dereferencing pointers under transient execution, the whole kernel’s address space can be brute-forced without crashing the system. Due to the adoption of PTI
[
33
]
, this kind of attack is mitigated by removing most of the kernel-space addresses from the page tables of user-space programs. The BlindSide attack
[
25
]
overcomes this issue by probing directly from kernel-space. Similar attacks can be mounted by triggering different forms of mispredictions
[
8
,
42
]
. Arm’s Pointer Authentication
[
52
]
is a technique that prevents forging pointers by extending them with an authentication code and raising an error if the code is violated. This can be used to deploy protections similar to layout randomization, but
Ravichandran et al. [50]
showed that by leveraging speculative execution, it is possible to brute-force the authentication codes.

Relation Between Security in the Speculative- and Classic-model

Blade
[
58
]
is a protection mechanism which is aimed at preventing speculative data-flows by selectively stopping speculations. The authors show that, with this mechanism, all those program that are constant-time in the sequential model, are constant-time in the speculative model too. This is similar to what we do in
Section

7
, by imposing
speculative kernel safety
on a system that enjoys
kernel safety
.
ProSpeCT

[
17
]
is an open-source RISC-V processor that ensures a similar guarantee: each program that is constant-time in the classical model remains
constant-time
even when executed on that processor. This protection relies on taint-tracking and requires explicit annotations on the security level of programs’ data.

Protections against Speculative Data Leaks

Commonly, speculative attacks are
aimed at leaking its victim’s secret data
[
34
,
38
,
17
,
58
,
28
,
29
,
10
,
14
]
. As a consequence,
many of the conventional mitigations against speculative attacks are aimed at
preventing secret data from leaking during speculative execution. For instance,
Speculative Load Hardening
[
13
]
is a software protection measure which,
in its simplest form, sets each value that is loaded from memory during transient execution
to a constant value. By doing so, this mechanism does not prevent these value to be loaded — i.e.
its application to the kernel would not prevent the attacker from breaking speculative kernel safety.
Together with the above-mentioned
ProSpeCT
, other hardware-level
taint-tracking based mechanisms have been deployed to prevent speculative leaks
[
61
,
60
]
. These mechanisms limit the speculative execution of
load instructions with different levels of strictness, ranging from completely preventing
the execution of these instructions (
[
60
]
, strict propagation and load restriction mode),
from just prohibiting the propagation of the loaded value
[
61
]
.
Although this approach is promising, the above-mentioned mechanisms do
not impose limitations on the speculative execution of indirect branches that may be used
by attackers in practice to break speculative kernel safety.

9
Conclusion

We have formally demonstrated that kernel’s layout randomization probabilistically ensures kernel safety for a classic model, where
an attacker cannot compromise the
system via speculative execution or side-channels, and
users of an operating system execute without privileges, but
victims can feature pointer arithmetic, introspection, and indirect jumps.

We have also shown that the protection offered by layout randomization does not naturally
scale against attackers that can control speculative execution and side-channels, and stipulate a sufficient condition
to enforce kernel safety in the Spectre era.
We also propose mechanisms based on program transformations that provably enforce
speculative kernel safety on a system, provided that this system
already enjoys kernel safety in the classic model. To the best of our knowledge, our work is the first to formally investigate and provide ways to achieve kernel safety in the presence of speculative and side-channel vulnerabilities.

This work prepares the ground for future developments such as
modeling more expressive attacker models, e.g.
attackers speculating on the branch target buffer, related to Spectre v2
[
34
]
,
optimizing the instrumentation we presented in
Section

7
,
and assessing its overhead on a real operating system.

An orthogonal research direction is to study more fine-grained
safety properties instead of
(speculative) kernel safety
—
e.g. by distinguishing violations of CFI from violations
of spatial safety, and data integrity from confidentiality, akin to what happens in
[
6
]
. In this direction, it would also be interesting to model
a call stack in order to determine whether the safety of kernel’s
stack can be granted under other, possibly weaker, conditions.

Finally, a valuable future development is to extend our execution model with other features
that are often used to undermine operating systems’ security, such as dynamic memory
allocation and dynamic module loading, with the aim to establish their impact on
system’s safety in presence of speculative attackers.

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
Appendix for
Section

5

A.1.1
Omitted Proofs and Results

We begin by introducing additional notation that will be used throughout this section. We then present the proof of
Theorem

1
, followed by the proofs of the intermediate results upon which this proof relies.

Semantics’ Notation

We write
{{\tau}[{({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},i)}\leftarrow{v}]}
to denote the store that is identical to
\tau
except for the array
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}}
, which remains pointwise equal to
\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}})
except at index
i
, where it is updated to
v
.

We use the notation
w\vdash_{\sigma}\phi{\downarrow}
when
\phi
does not reduce
in system
\sigma
and using layout
w
.

w\vdash_{\sigma}\phi\to^{!}\chi
to represent the formula

\exists n^{\prime}.w\vdash_{\sigma}\phi\to^{n^{\prime}}\chi\land w\vdash_{\sigma}\chi{\downarrow}
. Similarly, we use
w\vdash_{\sigma}\phi\to^{!n}\chi
to
denote the formula

\exists n^{\prime}\leq n.w\vdash_{\sigma}\phi\to^{n^{\prime}}\chi\land w\vdash_{\sigma}\chi{\downarrow}
.

Additionally, we generalize the function
\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}(\cdot)
to take full configurations as arguments. This allows us to use it in the following way:

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})\triangleq\begin{cases}\Omega&\text{if $w\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\,{\uparrow}$},\\
(v,\tau^{\prime})&\text{if }w\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{*}{(\langle\varepsilon,\rho[\mathit{ret}\mapsto v],b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})},\\
\mathsf{err}&\text{if }w\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{*}\mathsf{err},\\
\mathsf{unsafe}&\text{if }w\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{*}\mathsf{unsafe}.\end{cases}

We also extend the relation
\cong
by stating that:

(v,\tau)\cong{(\langle\varepsilon,\rho[\mathit{ret}\mapsto v],b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}

holds for every layout
w
, register map
\rho
, and every flag
b
. Furthermore, we take its symmetric and transitive closure.

User- and Kernel-mode stack

We write
\mathtt{u}(\mathtt{C})
as a shorthand for
\mathsf{ids}(\mathtt{C})\subseteq\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{u}}
. The predicate
\mathsf{ids}(F)
is defined inductively as follows:

\mathtt{u}(\epsilon)\triangleq\top\quad\mathtt{u}(f:F)\triangleq\mathtt{u}(f)\land\mathtt{u}(F)\quad\mathtt{u}(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle)\triangleq\mathtt{u}(\mathtt{C})\land b=\mathtt{u}.

The predicate
\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}
is defined analogously:

\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\epsilon)\triangleq\top\quad\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(f:F)\triangleq\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{t}}}}(f)\land\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(F)\quad\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle)\triangleq\mathtt{k}(\mathtt{C})\land b=\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}.

We write
\mathtt{k}(\cdot)
as a shorthand for
\exists{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}\in\mathsf{Sys}.\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\cdot)
.

Proof of
Theorem

1
.

Let
\sigma=(\tau,\gamma,\xi)
be a system. We aim to show that for every
unprivileged
command
\mathtt{C}
,
register map
\rho
, and layout distribution
\mu
, the following holds:

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})=\mathsf{unsafe}\right]=\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!}\mathsf{unsafe}\right]\leq 1-\delta_{\mu}.

If the probability is not 0, by
Lemma

4
(that we can apply because all the system calls in
\sigma
are
layout non-interferent
, and because all the layouts are identical with respect to user-space identifiers), we deduce that:

\forall w.\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})\cong\mathsf{unsafe}.

This proposition can be rewritten as:

\forall w.\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})\in\{\mathsf{unsafe},\mathsf{err}\},

which allows us to deduce that:

\forall w.\exists\chi_{w},n_{w}.w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!n_{w}}\chi_{w}.

Since memory size is finite, also
\mathsf{Layout}
is finite, so there is a natural number
\overline{n}
such that
\overline{n}\geq\max_{w\in\mathsf{Layout}}{n_{w}}
.
Thus, we obtain:

\forall w.\exists\chi_{w}.w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!\overline{n}}\chi_{w}.

Applying
Lemma

5
to the system
\sigma
, the
bound
\overline{n}
, the unprivileged attacker
\mathtt{C}
, the
initial register map
\rho
, the distribution
\mu
, and to the last
intermediate claim, we deduce that one of the following statements
holds:

–

There are a register map
\rho^{\prime}
and a store
\tau^{\prime}=_{\mathsf{Fun}}\tau

such that for every layout
w
:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!\overline{n}}{(\langle\varepsilon,\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})},

–

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!\overline{n}}\mathsf{err}\right]\geq\delta_{\mu}.

We go by cases on these two statements. If the first one holds,
then for every layout
w
:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!}{(\langle\varepsilon,\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}.

Consequently, we have

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!}{(\langle\varepsilon,\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\right]=1.

Since the final configuration cannot be contemporary
\mathsf{unsafe}

and
{{(\langle\varepsilon,\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}
, we conclude:

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!}\mathsf{unsafe}\right]=0.

This established the claim. If the second proposition holds then:

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!}\mathsf{err}\right]\geq\\
\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!\overline{n}}\mathsf{err}\right]\geq\delta_{\mu},

and therefore we conclude that:

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!}\mathsf{unsafe}\right]\leq\\
1-\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!}\mathsf{err}\right]\leq 1-\delta_{\mu}.

∎

Lemma 4

(Preservation of
layout non-interference
)
.

If every system call in
\sigma
is
layout non-interferent
,
then the entire system is also
layout non-interferent
with
respect to
unprivileged
attackers, in the following sense:
for every
unprivileged
attacker
\mathtt{C}
, register map

\rho
, pair of layouts
w_{1},w_{2}
, and configuration

\phi_{1}
we have that:

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{1}}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})\cong\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{2}}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})

Proof.

The main claim is a consequence of an auxiliary claim, namely:

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!}\phi_{1}\Rightarrow\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{2}}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})\cong\phi_{1}

(C)

To prove (C), we establish a slightly stronger statement: instead of
quantifying over
\mathtt{C}
and
\rho
, we generalize to a non-empty
stack
F
such that
\mathtt{u}(F)
, and we rewrite the claim in a more
convenient shape, where all the meta-variables that are not
quantified explicitly are quantified universally.

\forall n.\forall\tau^{\prime}=_{\mathsf{Fun}}\tau.\left(w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{n}\phi_{1}\land w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}\phi_{1}\not\to\right)\Rightarrow\\
\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{2}}({(F,w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})})\cong\phi_{1}

(C’)

Once (C’) is established, we derive (C) by instantiating
\tau^{\prime}
as

\tau
and
F
as

{{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}}
. We fix

n
as the number of steps taken by the reduction from the
initial configuration to the terminal configuration

\phi_{1}
. From (C), we can deduce the main claim by case
analysis:

-

Case

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{1}}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})=\Omega
. In this case, the value of

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{2}}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})
must also be
\Omega
. If this was not
the case, we could apply (C) with
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
swapped to
prove that

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{1}}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})\neq\Omega
.

-

Case

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{1}}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})\neq\Omega
. In this case, the
conclusion is a direct consequence of (C).

Now that we showed how the main claim can be deduced from (C’), we
can focus on the proof of (C’). In the proof we will extensively
use the assumption on the layouts:

\forall{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}\in\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{u}}.w_{1}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}})=w_{2}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}),

For this reason, we fix it on top and name it (†). The proof goes by induction on
n
.

-

Case
0. We assume

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{0}\phi_{1}\land w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}\phi_{1}\not\to.

This implies that
\phi_{1}={(F,w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}
. From this last observation, and since
\phi_{1}
is
terminal, we deduce that
F=\langle\varepsilon,\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle
. By
examining the semantics, we also establish that

w_{2}\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\not\to,

so we conclude that

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{2}}({(F,w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})})=(\rho^{\prime}(\mathit{ret}),\tau^{\prime})\cong\phi_{1}
.

-

Case

n+1
.
Observe that
F\neq\epsilon
, as it would contradict the assumption

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{n+1}\phi_{1}\land w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}\phi_{1}\not\to.

Thus, we assume
F=\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime}
, and proceed by case analysis on
\mathtt{C}
.
Note that the induction hypothesis coincides syntactically with (C’).

-

Case

\varepsilon
. In this case, we rewrite the first part of (H) as follows:

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\varepsilon,\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to{(F^{\prime\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{n}{\phi_{1}},

where
F^{\prime\prime}
is obtained by updating the topmost register map
in
F^{\prime}
with the return value from
\rho
. Since

\mathtt{u}(F^{\prime})
holds, also
\mathtt{u}(st^{\prime\prime})
does, so we can apply the IH
and conclude that

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{2}}({(F^{\prime\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})})\cong\phi_{1}
. Thus, to conclude the proof, it suffices
to observe that

w_{2}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\varepsilon,\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to{{(F^{\prime\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}},

which follows by introspection of the semantics.

-

Case

x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\mathtt{E}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. In this case, we rewrite
the first part of (H) as follows:

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\mathtt{E}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{x}\leftarrow{\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{1}}}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}\to^{n}{\phi_{1}}

observe that

\mathtt{u}(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{x}\leftarrow{\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{1}}}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime})
holds, so we can apply the IH and
conclude that

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{2}}({(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{x}\leftarrow{\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{1}}}]},,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})})\cong\phi_{1}

This means that, in order to conclude the proof, it suffices to observe that

w_{2}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\mathtt{E}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{x}\leftarrow{\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{1}}}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}},

which, in turn, reduces to showing that

\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{1}}=\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{2}}
. By
definition of
\mathtt{u}(F)
, we have that

x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\mathtt{E}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
is an unprivileged command, so, in
particular all identifiers in
\mathtt{E}
belong to
\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{u}}
, hence,
the
\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{1}}=\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{2}}

follows from
Remark

2
, and (†).

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{skip}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. Analogous to the case of
assignments.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{if}}}\ \mathtt{E}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{then}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}_{\bot}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{else}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}_{\top}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fi}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
.
Analogous to the case of assignments.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{while}}}\ \mathtt{E}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{do}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{od}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. Analogous to the
case of assignments.

-

Case

{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. In this case,
we start by observing that

\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{1}}=\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{2}}
by

Remark

2
. This also implies

\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{1}}^{\mathsf{Addr}}=\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{2}}^{\mathsf{Addr}}
. Let

\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{1}}^{\mathsf{Addr}}=p
. By

Remark

2
, we also know that

\llbracket\mathtt{F}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{1}}=\llbracket\mathtt{F}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{2}}
, and we denote this value
v
.
From the assumption (†) we deduce that

\underline{w}_{1}(\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}})=\underline{w}_{2}(\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}})
, and
we denote this set
P
. We go by cases on
p\in P
.

-

Case

p\in P
. In this case, the rule

applies to both the configurations

{{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}

and

{{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}

under the layouts
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
respectively, obtaining:

{{(\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},{w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}[{p}\leftarrow{v}]})}}

and

{{(\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},{w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}[{p}\leftarrow{v}]})}}.

Since
p\in\underline{w}_{1}(\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}})

and by definition of
\underline{w}_{1}
, we deduce that
there exist
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}}\in\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}}

and
0\leq i\leq\mathsf{size}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}})
such that
w_{1}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}})+i=p
.
By (†), we also deduce that
w_{2}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}})+i=p
,
so we can apply
Remark

3

in order to show that:

{w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}[{p}\leftarrow{v}]}=w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{\tau^{\prime}[{({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},i)}\leftarrow{v}]}

and that

{w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}[{p}\leftarrow{v}]}=w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}{\tau^{\prime}[{({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},i)}\leftarrow{v}]}.

Finally, since
\mathtt{u}({\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime}})
holds
and we have:

{\tau^{\prime}[{({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},i)}\leftarrow{v}]}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}{\tau^{\prime}}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau,

we can apply the IH, and conclude the proof of this
sub-derivation.

-

Case

p\notin P
. In this case,
from (†), we deduce that

p\notin\underline{w}_{2}(\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}})
. Therefore, the
rule
applies, showing both:

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\mathsf{err},

and

w_{2}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\mathsf{err}.

This establishes the claim.

-

Case

x\ \mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\text{{*}}{\mathtt{E}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. Analogous to the
case of memory store operations.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. This case is also similar to that of memory loads,
but requires some non-trivial observations. As before, we
define
p=\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{1}}^{\mathsf{Addr}}
and observe
that
p=\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{2}}^{\mathsf{Addr}}
because of

Remark

2
. Similarly, we introduce the values

v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
which correspond to the semantics of

\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}
evaluated under
\rho
and both
layouts
w_{1},w_{2}
. By (†), we have

w_{1}(\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}})=w_{2}(\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}})
, so we denote this set

P_{\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
, and we go by case analysis on

p\in P_{\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
.

-

Case

p\in P_{\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
. In this case, there exists

{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}\in\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}
such that
w_{1}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=p
, and from
(†) we deduce
p=w_{2}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})
. Using these intermediate
conclusions and the definition of
\mathrel{\diamond}
, we deduce that

(w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})(p)=\tau^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})
, (the
last step follows from the assumption
\tau^{\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau
),
and similarly for
w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}(p)
. Since

p\in P_{\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
, we deduce that rule
can
be applied, proving:

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}},

and

w_{2}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}},

where
\rho_{0}^{\prime}=\rho_{0}[x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\leftarrow v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
.
Finally, by assumption on
\tau
, we know that
\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})
is an unprivileged program. So, in particular,

\mathtt{u}({\langle\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime}})

holds. This allows us to apply the IH and conclude the proof.

-

Case

p\notin P_{\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
. Analogous to the corresponding case for store operations.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{E}_{1},\ldots,\mathtt{E}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. This is perhaps the most interesting
case of this proof. We begin by introducing the values

v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
, which correspond to the
semantics of
\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}
evaluated under

\rho
as well as any of layouts
w_{1},w_{2}
.These
values do not depend on the layout because of
Remark

2
. By
introspection of the rule
, we deduce that
both the following statements hold:

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}},

and

w_{2}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}},

where

\rho_{0}^{\prime}=\rho_{0}[x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\leftarrow v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
. Unfortunately, we cannot apply the IH
to the configuration

{{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}

because
\mathtt{u}(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime})
does not hold. However, we can go by cases on

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{1}}({{\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}})},{\rho_{0}^{\prime}},{\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}},\tau^{\prime}})

-

Case

\Omega
. By
Lemma

7
, we deduce that

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{1}}({(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})})=\Omega,

which leads to a contradiction because we assumed

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{n+1}\phi_{1}\land w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}\phi_{1}{\downarrow}_{.}

-

Case

\mathsf{unsafe}
. From the
layout non-intreference property
(
Definition

2
), we deduce that

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{2}}({\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}},\tau^{\prime}})\in\{\mathsf{err},\mathsf{unsafe}\}.

We take
\mathsf{err}
as an example. This means that

\exists h.w_{2}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{h}\mathsf{err}.

By applying
Lemma

7
to the reduction with
w_{1}
,
we deduce that

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{n}\mathsf{unsafe}.

By applying
Lemma

7
to the
reduction with
w_{2}
, we deduce that:

w_{2}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},b\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!}\mathsf{err}.

We conclude by observing that
\mathsf{unsafe}\cong\mathsf{err}
. The case with
\mathsf{unsafe}
follows analogously.

-

Case

\mathsf{err}
. Analogous to the previous case.

-

Case

(v,m)
.
We start by observing that
m=w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime}
for some
\tau^{\prime\prime}
due to
Remark

4
. Form the
layout non-interference

property (
Definition

2
),
we deduce that

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{2}}({\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}})},{\rho_{0}^{\prime}},{\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}},\tau^{\prime})={(v,\tau^{\prime\prime})}.

By applying
Lemma

7
twice, we deduce that:

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}\to^{*}\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{\mathit{ret}}\leftarrow{v}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}}

and

w_{2}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}\to^{*}\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{\mathit{ret}}\leftarrow{v}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}}

Next, we observe that we assumed

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{n+1}{\phi_{1}},

and
\phi_{1}
is terminal, so it must be the case that

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{\mathit{ret}}\leftarrow{v}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}\to^{!}\phi_{1}
. In particular, the
reduction requires less than
n+1
steps. From the
assumption
\mathtt{u}(F)
, we also deduce
\mathtt{u}(F^{\prime})
, and

\mathtt{u}(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{\mathit{ret}}\leftarrow{v}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime})
and
from
Remark

4
we conclude that

\tau^{\prime\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau^{\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau
, so we can apply the IH
to

{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{\mathit{ret}}\leftarrow{v}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}}

and deduce that

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w_{2}}({(\langle\varepsilon,\rho^{\prime},b_{2}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})})\cong\phi_{1},

which concludes the proof.

∎

Lemma 5

(Main Lemma)
.

Let
\sigma=(\tau,\gamma,\xi)
be a
layout non-interferent
system.
For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

unprivileged
program
\mathtt{C}
,
register map
\rho
, and distribution
\mu
,
if

\forall w\in\mathsf{Layout}.\exists\chi_{w}.w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!n}\chi_{w},

then one of the two following statements must hold:

–

There are a register map
\rho^{\prime}
and a store
\tau^{\prime}=_{\mathsf{Fun}}\tau

such that, for every layout
w
, we have the reduction

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!n}{(\langle\varepsilon,\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}.

–

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{!n}\mathsf{err}\right]\geq\delta_{\mu}.

Proof.

To facilitate the induction, we prove a slightly stronger statement. Specifically, we replace the quantification over
\mathtt{C}
and
\rho
with a quantification over a non-empty stack
F
such that
\mathtt{u}(F)
holds. Additionally, we quantify over a general
\tau^{\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau
instead of
\tau
, and we rewrite the claim in a more convenient form. In the following claim, all meta-variables that are not explicitly quantified are globally and universally quantified. As a result, we obtain the following claim:
if
\forall w\in\mathsf{Layout}.\exists\chi_{w}.w\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n}\chi_{w}
, then:

–

\exists\rho^{\prime},\tau^{\prime\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau^{\prime}.\forall w\in\mathsf{Layout}.w\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n}{{(\langle\varepsilon,\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}}
, or

–

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n}\mathsf{err}\right]\geq\delta_{\mu}
.

We call this auxiliary claim (C’) and we proceed by induction on
n
.

-

Case
0. In this case, we assume
