\forall w\in\mathsf{Layout}.\exists\chi_{w}.w\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!0}\chi_{w},

and from this premise, we deduce that

{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}
is terminal. Using the definition
of the notation
\to^{!}
, the fact that
\mathtt{u}(F)
holds, and the
assumption on the non-emptiness of
F
, we conclude that

F=\langle\varepsilon,\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:\epsilon
for some
\rho
. Hence, the claim
holds trivially by choosing the register map
\rho
and the
store
\tau^{\prime}
.

-

Case

n+1
. In this case, we start by assuming

\forall w\in\mathsf{Layout}.\exists\chi_{w}.w\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n+1}\chi_{w}

and we go by cases on
F
, excluding the case where
F=\epsilon
due to our assumption on the non-emptiness of
F
.
Therefore, in the following, we assume that
F=\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime}
,
and we proceed by cases on
\mathtt{C}
. Notice that the induction
hypothesis coincides syntactically with (C’).

-

Case

x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\mathtt{E}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. In this case, we observe
that there exists a value
v
such that, for every layout

w
, we have
\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}=v
. More
specifically, this follows from
Remark

2
, since for
every
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}\in\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{u}}
and every pair of layouts
w_{1},w_{2}
,
it holds that
w_{1}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}})=w_{2}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}})
. In particular, all
identifiers appearing in
\mathtt{E}
belong to
\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{u}}
.
By introspection of the semantics, we deduce that for every layout
w
:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\mathtt{E}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{x}\leftarrow{v}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}.

(
*
)

Notice that

\mathtt{u}(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{x}\leftarrow{v}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime})
. Because of (H) and this observation, we
can apply the IH to the stack in the target configuration and

\tau^{\prime}
to conclude that one among (A) and (B) below holds.

\exists\rho^{\prime},\tau^{\prime\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau^{\prime}.\forall w\in\mathsf{Layout}.\\
w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{x}\leftarrow{v}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n}\\
{{(\langle\varepsilon,\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}}

(A)

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{x}\leftarrow{v}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n}\mathsf{err}\right]\geq\delta_{\mu}.

We go by cases on this disjunction.

-

Case
A. In this case, we introduce
\rho^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}
from the IH,
we assume
\tau^{\prime\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau^{\prime}
, we fix a layout
w
and from
(
*
) and (A), we conclude:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\mathtt{E}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{n+1}{{(\langle\varepsilon,\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}}.

Due to the generality of
w
, we can
introduce universal quantification over all layouts, thereby
completing the proof.

-

Case
B. We call
E
the set of all the layouts
\overline{w}
such that

\overline{w}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{x}\leftarrow{v}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n}\mathsf{err}

Notice that we can apply the assumption (
*
)
to each of these layouts, showing that

\forall\overline{w}\in E.\overline{w}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\mathtt{E}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},\overline{w}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n+1}\mathsf{err}.

From (B), we know that the probability associated to the event
E
is bigger than
\delta_{\mu}
,
the last observation establishes the claim.

-

Case

\varepsilon
. Analogous to the previous case.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{skip}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. Analogous to the case of
assignments.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{if}}}\ \mathtt{E}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{then}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}_{\bot}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{else}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}_{\top}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fi}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
.
Analogous to the case of assignments.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{while}}}\ \mathtt{E}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{do}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{od}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. Analogous to the
case of assignments.

-

Case

{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. In this case,
we begin by observing that there exists a value
v_{\mathtt{E}}

such that for every
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
, we have

\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}=v_{\mathtt{E}}
. This follows from

Remark

2
, since we assume that

\mathtt{u}({\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D})
, which implies that
all identifiers within
\mathtt{E}
belong to
\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{u}}
.
For the same reason, there exists a unique value
v_{\mathtt{F}}

such that for every layout
w
, we have

\llbracket\mathtt{F}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}=v_{\mathtt{F}}
. Consequently,
there is a unique address
p\in\mathsf{Addr}
such that

p=v^{\mathsf{Addr}}
.
Similarly, we deduce that there exists a unique set of addresses

P_{\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
such that, for every layout
w
, we have

\underline{w}(\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}})=P_{\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
. Moreover, due to the
assumptions on the set of layouts, we conclude that

P_{\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}}}\subseteq\mathsf{Addr}_{\mathtt{u}}
.
Finally, we proceed by cases on whether
p\in P_{\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
.

-

Case

p\in P_{\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
. The rule

can be applied to each configuration

{{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}

for every layout
w
, obtaining:

{{(\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},{w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}[{p}\leftarrow{v_{\mathtt{E}}}]})}};

from the assumption
p\in\underline{w}(\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}})

and the definition of
\underline{w}
, we deduce that
there is
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}}\in\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}}

and
0\leq i\leq\mathsf{size}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}})
such that
w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}})+i=p

and that for every other layout
\overline{w}

it must hold that
\overline{w}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}})+i=p
,
so we can apply
Remark

3

in order to show that

\forall w\in\mathsf{Layout}.{w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}[{p}\leftarrow{v_{\mathtt{E}}}]}=w\mathrel{\diamond}{\tau^{\prime}[{({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},i)}\leftarrow{v_{\mathtt{E}}}]}.

Finally, we observe that
\mathtt{u}({\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime}})
holds
and that

{\tau^{\prime}[{({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},i)}\leftarrow{v_{\mathtt{E}}}]}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}{\tau^{\prime}}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau

holds as well, so we can apply the IH
and conclude the proof of this sub-derivation as we did in the case of assignments.

-

Case

p\notin P_{\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
.
In this case,
we just observe that
the rule

can be applied to show both

w_{1}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{1}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\mathsf{err},

and

w_{2}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w_{2}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\mathsf{err}.

This establishes the claim.

-

Case

x\ \mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\text{{*}}{\mathtt{E}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. Analogous to the case of store operations.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
.
This case is similar to that of stores but requires additional
considerations regarding the stacks of the target configurations.
We begin by observing that there exists a unique address
p

such that for every
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
, we have
\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}^{\mathsf{Addr}}=p
. Similarly, we define the values

v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
, which correspond to the semantics of

\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}
evaluated under
\rho
for every
layout
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
.
Next, we note the existence of a set
P_{\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
such that
for all
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
, we have
\underline{w}(\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}})=P_{\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
.
The proof proceeds by cases on
p\in P_{\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
.

-

Case

p\in P_{\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
. In this case, there exists
a unique
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}\in\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}
such that for every

w\in\mathsf{Layout}
, we have
w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=p
. From this
observation and the definition of
\mathrel{\diamond}
, we deduce that for
every
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
,
(w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})(p)=\tau^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})
,
where the last step follows from the assumption
\tau^{\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau
. Since

p\in P_{\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
, we deduce that:

\forall p\in P_{\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}}.w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}},

where

\rho_{0}^{\prime}=\rho_{0}[x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\leftarrow v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
. Finally, we observe that

\mathtt{u}(\langle\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime})

holds since
\tau
maps unprivileged commands to identifiers
in
\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}
. This allows us to apply the IH. The proof then
follows the same structure as in the case of assignments.

-

Case

p\notin P_{\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}}
. Analogous to the
corresponding case for store operations.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{E}_{1},\ldots,\mathtt{E}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
.
We begin by introducing the values
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
, which correspond to the semantics of
\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}
evaluated under
\rho
and all layouts
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
. By introspection of the rule
, we deduce that for every
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
, the following holds:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}},

(†)

where
\rho_{0}^{\prime}=\rho_{0}[x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\leftarrow v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
. However, we cannot apply the IH on the configuration

{{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}

because

\mathtt{u}(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime})

does not hold. Nevertheless, we can apply

Lemma

2
and deduce that
one of the following statements must hold:

\exists\overline{\rho},\overline{\tau}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau^{\prime}.\forall w\in\mathsf{Layout}.\\
w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,{w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}})}\to^{!}{(\langle\varepsilon,\overline{\rho},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})},

(A)

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!}\mathsf{err}\right]\geq\delta_{\mu},

\forall w\in\mathsf{Layout}.\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})})=\Omega.

We go by cases on the valid statement.

-

Case
A. From (A) and
Lemma

7
, we conclude that
there exist a register map
\overline{\rho}
and a store

\overline{\tau}
such that for every
layout
w
, we have:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!}\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{\mathit{ret}}\leftarrow{\overline{\rho}(\mathit{ret})}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\overline{\tau})}}.

We also observe that
\tau=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau^{\prime}
by
Remark

4
. Combining this with (†), we conclude:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!}\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{\mathit{ret}}\leftarrow{\overline{\rho}(\mathit{ret})}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\overline{\tau})}}

holds as well.
From (H), we also deduce that the number of steps must be at most
n+1
, while from (†), we infer that it must be greater than 1. This allows us to apply the IH to

{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{\mathit{ret}}\leftarrow{\overline{\rho}(\mathit{ret})}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\overline{\tau})}}
. The proof then proceeds similarly to the case of assignments.

-

Case
B. Define
E
as the set of all layouts
\overline{w}\in\mathsf{Layout}
such that:

\overline{w}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,\overline{w}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!}\mathsf{err}

Since (†) holds for each of these layouts, it follows that
for every
\overline{w}\in E
, we have:

\overline{w}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},\overline{w}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!}\mathsf{err}.

From (B), we know that the probability measure associated with
this set is greater than
\delta_{\mu}
. Furthermore, from (H),
we deduce:

\overline{w}\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u},:\rangle F^{\prime},\overline{w}\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n+1}\mathsf{err},

which completes the claim.

-

Case
C. Let
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
be any layout.
From
Lemma

7
, we deduce that

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})})=\Omega,

which contradicts our assumption (H). This completes the proof.

The main claim is a particular case of C’ where
F
is
\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle
, and
\tau^{\prime}
is
\tau
.
∎

See
2

Proof.

We begin by fixing all the universally quantified variables in the
statement using the same meta-variable as before. We also fix some

\overline{w}\in\mathsf{Layout}
and proceed by case analysis on

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,\overline{w}}({(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}).

-

Case

(v,\tau^{\prime\prime})
.
In this case, by the
layout non-interference
property, we conclude that (A) holds for
\overline{v}=v
and
\overline{\tau}=\tau^{\prime\prime}
. Furthermore, by
Remark

4
, we know that
\tau^{\prime\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau^{\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau
.

-

Case

\mathsf{unsafe},\mathsf{err}
.
Here, by the
layout non-interference
property, we deduce that for every
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
,

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})})\in\{\mathsf{err},\mathsf{unsafe}\}.

Consequently, for every
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
, there exists some
n_{w}
such that

\exists n_{w}.w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{n_{w}}\chi\in\{\mathsf{err},\mathsf{unsafe}\}.

Since
\mathsf{Layout}
is finite, there exists a uniform bound
\overline{n}
such that

\forall w\in\mathsf{Layout}.w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!\overline{n}}\chi\in\{\mathsf{err},\mathsf{unsafe}\}.

From this, we conclude that

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!\overline{n}}\mathsf{err}\right]\geq\delta_{\mu}.

This follows by considering an enumeration
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{0},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{h}
of the identifiers in
\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))
and noting that the probability above can be rewritten as:

\sum_{p_{1},\dots,p_{h}\in\mathsf{Addr}_{\mathtt{k}}}\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\Big[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!\overline{n}}\mathsf{err}\,\,\Big|\,\,\forall 1\leq i\leq h.w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i})=p_{i}\Big]\cdot\\
\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}[w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{1})=p_{1},\dots,w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{h})=p_{h}]

From (†) and
Lemma

1
, we deduce that for every
choice of
p_{1},\dots,p_{h}\in\mathsf{Addr}_{\mathtt{k}}
, the terms on the left
in the expression above are bounded by
\delta_{\mu}
making
their convex combination bounded by
\delta_{\mu}
. moreover, since:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!\overline{n}}\mathsf{err}\Rightarrow w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!}\mathsf{err}

for the definition of
\to^{!m}
, and this means that

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!}\mathsf{err}\right]\geq\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\left[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!\overline{n}}\mathsf{err}\right],

which establishes the claim.

-

Case

\Omega
. In this case, due to the
layout non-interference
property, we conclude that (C) holds.

∎

For the next lemma, we extend
\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}
to frame stacks as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\triangleq\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(f\mathrel{:}F)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(f)\cup\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(F)

\displaystyle\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{C}).

See
1

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
.

-

Case
0.
In this case, we conclude that either (A) or (C) holds trivially, depending on whether
\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}})\neq\varepsilon
or not.

-

Case

n+1
.
We begin by applying the IH to the initial configuration. Three cases arise:

-

Case
A. Let
\sigma
be a system,
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}
a system call
and consider the initial configuration

{{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}
. Suppose

\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))\setminus\mathsf{Sys}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{h}\}
and fix addresses
p_{1},\dots,p_{h}
. By the IH,
there exists a stack
F
and a
store
\tau^{\prime\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau
satisfying the following property:

\forall w.\left(\forall 1\leq i\leq h,w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i})=p_{i}\right)\Rightarrow w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{n}{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}.

Since
F
is non-empty, we can assume that
F=\langle\mathtt{C},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, from the IH, we know that that
\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(F)\subseteq\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))
(H). We now perform a case analysis on
\mathtt{C}
.

-

Case

x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\mathtt{E}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. We first observe that
there exists a value
v
such that, for every layout in our
quantification
w
,
\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}=v
.
This follows from
Remark

2
, given that for every

{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}\in\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))
and any two layouts

w_{1},w_{2}
, we assume that
w_{1}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}})=w_{2}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}})
. In
particular, this applies to identifiers appearing in
\mathtt{E}

that belong to
\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))
by (H). For all
layouts if our quantification layouts, we have:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\mathtt{E}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho^{\prime}[{x}\leftarrow{v}]},\mathtt{k}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}}.

(
*
)

From (H), we deduce

\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho^{\prime}[{x}\leftarrow{v}]},\mathtt{k}\rangle:F^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))

Therefore, we conclude that (A) holds if either

\mathtt{D}\neq\varepsilon
or
F^{\prime}
is non-empty. Otherwise, (C)
holds.

-

Case

\varepsilon
. Analogous to the case above.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{skip}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. Analogous to the case of
assignments.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{if}}}\ \mathtt{E}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{then}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}_{\bot}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{else}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}_{\top}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fi}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
.
Analogous to the case of assignments.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{while}}}\ \mathtt{E}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{do}}}\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{C}\end{array}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{od}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. Analogous to the
case of assignments.

-

Case

{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. In this case,
we start by observing that there exist a value
v_{\mathtt{E}}
such
that for each layout
w
that satisfies the premise, we have

\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}=v_{\mathtt{E}}
. This follows from

Remark

2
, (H2), and the definition of
\mathsf{refs}
, which
ensures that all the identifiers within
\mathtt{E}
are in

\mathsf{refs}(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime})
.
For the same reason, there is a unique value
v_{\mathtt{F}}

such that for each of the layout in our quantification, we have
that
\llbracket\mathtt{F}\rrbracket_{\rho^{\prime},w}=v_{\mathtt{F}}
.
Therefore, there is a unique
p\in\mathsf{Addr}=v_{\mathtt{E}}^{\mathsf{Addr}}
which
the store instruction attempts to write at. Finally, we observe
that there is a unique set
P
such that, for every layout

w
satisfying the assumption, we have

P=\underline{w}(\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))\setminus\mathsf{Sys})
. We go by
case analysis on
p\in P
.

-

Case

p\in P
. In this case, for every layout
w

that we are quantifying over, there exists some index
i
such
that
p\in\underline{w}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i})
. If
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i}
is a
procedure identifier, then we have

p=\underline{w}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i})
, and thus, independently of
w
, we have:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}\to\mathsf{err}.

This shows that (B) holds with probability 1. Otherwise,
there exists a unique array
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}}
and a unique index
j
such
that, independently of the layout, we have

w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}})+j=p
. Notice that if
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}}
and
j
were not
unique, then it would not be true that the layouts store

{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{h}
respectively at
p_{1},\dots,p_{h}
.
Thus, each layout
w
that satisfies the premises of rule

, we apply the rule to show the following
transition:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},{(w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})[{p}\leftarrow{v_{\mathtt{F}}}]})}}.

Using
Remark

3
, we further conclude:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}{({\tau^{\prime\prime}[{({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},j)}\leftarrow{v_{\mathtt{F}}}]})})}}.

By observing the uniqueness of the target configuration modulo

w
, and given that

\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\langle\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))
follows from (H), we
conclude that (A) holds. If
\mathtt{D}=\varepsilon
and
F^{\prime}
is
empty (C) holds, instead.

-

Case

p\notin P
.
Observe that, for every layout
w
such that

p\notin\underline{w}(\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{k}})
,
only rule
applies, which shows the following transition:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}\to\mathsf{err}.

Thus, we observe that:

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\big[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}\to\mathsf{err}\,\,\big|\\
\forall 1\leq i\leq h.w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i})=p_{i}\big]

is greater than

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\big[p\notin\underline{w}(\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{k}})\mid\forall 1\leq i\leq h.w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i})=p_{i}\big]

which, by definition, is greater than
\delta_{\mu}
.
This shows that (B) holds.

-

Case

x\ \mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\text{{*}}{\mathtt{E}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. Analogous to the
case of store operations.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
. We start by observing that there exists a unique
address
p
such that for every
w
that satisfies the
precondition, we have
\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}^{\mathsf{Addr}}=p
. Similarly, we introduce the
values
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
, which correspond to the semantics
of
\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}
evaluated under
\rho
and
every layout that satisfies the precondition. Then, we observe
that there exists a set
P
such that for each layout under
consideration, it holds that

\underline{w}(\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))\setminus\mathsf{Sys})=P
. The proof proceeds by cases on whether
p\in P
.

-

Case

p\in P
. In this case, there is a unique identifier
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{j}
such that for every layout
w
that satisfies the precondition, we have
p\in\underline{w}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{j})
. We analyze two cases based on whether
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{j}
is a function identifier
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}
.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{j}={\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}
. In this case, from the definition
of
\mathrel{\diamond}
, we deduce that for each of these layouts we
have
w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime}(p)=\tau^{\prime\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})
,
where the last step follows from the assumption

\tau^{\prime\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau
. Since
p\in P
, we conclude
that, independently of the specific layout, if the
preconditions hold, then:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}},

where
\rho_{0}^{\prime}=\rho_{0}[x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\leftarrow v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
.
By definition,
\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))

contains all the identifiers within

\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{j})
because
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{j}\in\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))
and

\mathsf{refs}
is closed under procedure calls.
This shows that (A) holds.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{j}={\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}}
. In this case, since the set of
array identifiers and that of functions are disjoint, we
conclude that for every layout
w
that satisfies the
preconditions, we have that

p\notin\underline{w}(\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{k}})
. This means that
for each of these layouts, we can show:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime})}\to\mathsf{err},

and this means that (B) holds with probability 1.

-

Case

p\notin P
. Analogous to the corresponding case
for store operations.

-

Case

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}
.
Analogous to the previous case.

-

Case
B. From the definition of
\to^{!n}
, we observe that if

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n}\mathsf{err},

then

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n+1}\mathsf{err}.

This implies that

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\Big[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n+1}\mathsf{err}\,\,\Big|\,\,\forall 1\leq i\leq h.w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i})=p_{i}\Big]

is greater than

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\mu}\Big[w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to^{!n}\mathsf{err}\,\,\Big|\,\,\forall 1\leq i\leq h.w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i})=p_{i}\Big],

which proves the claim.

-

Case
C. Similar to the previous case.

∎

Remark 1
.

\delta_{\nu}\geq\min_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}\in\mathsf{Sys}}\frac{\kappa_{\mathtt{k}}/\theta-|{\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{k}}}|}{\kappa_{\mathtt{k}}/\theta-|{\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))\setminus\mathsf{Sys}}|}

Proof.

We want to prove that

\min\bigl\{\displaystyle{\Pr_{w\leftarrow\nu}}[p\notin\underline{w}(\mathsf{Id})\mid w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i}^{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}})=p_{i},\text{ for }1\leq i\leq h]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}\in\mathsf{Sys},p,p_{1},\dots,p_{h}\in\mathsf{Addr}_{\mathtt{k}}\land{}\\[3.0pt]
\phantom{{}\mid}p\notin\{p_{i},\ldots,p_{i}+\mathsf{size}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i}^{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}})-1\},\text{ for }1\leq i\leq h\bigr\}.

is greater than

\min_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}\in\mathsf{Sys}}\frac{\kappa_{\mathtt{k}}/\theta-|{\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{k}}}|}{\kappa_{\mathtt{k}}/\theta-|{\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))}\setminus\mathsf{Sys}|}
.
To establish this, we fix a system call
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}\in\mathsf{Sys}

and addresses

p,p_{1},\dots,p_{h}\in\mathsf{Addr}_{\mathtt{k}}
that achieve the minimum.
In particular, we have:

p\notin\{p_{i},\ldots,p_{i}+\mathsf{size}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i}^{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}})-1\},\text{ for }1\leq i\leq h.

Given that the minimum exists,
p_{1},\ldots,p_{h}
are the starting
addresses of the slots
s_{1},\ldots,s_{h}
where the references

\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))\setminus\mathsf{Sys}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}
are allocated.
Now, assume that
p
is located within one of these slots, say

s_{j}
. From assumption (†), we deduce that
p
cannot be
allocated, since no object other than
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{j}
can be placed in that
slot. As a result, we obtain:

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\nu}[p\notin\underline{w}(\mathsf{Id})\mid w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i}^{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}})=p_{i},\text{ for }1\leq i\leq h]=1,

which establishes the claim.

If, on the other hand,
p
is located in a different slot
s
, we observe that:

\Pr_{w\leftarrow\nu}[p\notin\underline{w}(\mathsf{Id})\mid w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i}^{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}})=p_{i},\text{ for }1\leq i\leq h]\geq\\
\Pr_{w\leftarrow\nu}[\mathit{slotof}(p)\notin w(\mathsf{Id})\mid w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i}^{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}})=p_{i},\text{ for }1\leq i\leq h]

where
\mathit{slotof}
associates each address with the starting
address of its corresponding slot.
Due to the definition of
\nu
, the slots are sampled uniformly and
they are independent. Therefore, the probability to the right in
(
*
) is given by the ratio of the free slots and those that are not
occupied by elements of

\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))\setminus\mathsf{Sys}
, because of the
assumption (†). In conclusion (
*
) is equal to:

\frac{\kappa_{\mathtt{k}}/\theta-|{\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{k}}}|}{\kappa_{\mathtt{k}}/\theta-|{\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))}\setminus\mathsf{Sys}|}\geq\min_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}\in\mathsf{Sys}}\frac{\kappa_{\mathtt{k}}/\theta-|{\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{k}}}|}{\kappa_{\mathtt{k}}/\theta-|{\mathsf{refs}_{\sigma}(\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))}\setminus\mathsf{Sys}|}.

∎

Technical observations

Remark 2
.

Let
\{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{h}\}\subseteq\mathsf{Id}
be a set of identifiers,
\{p_{1},\dots,p_{h}\}
a set of addresses, and
W\subseteq\{w\in\mathsf{Layout}\mid\forall 1\leq i\leq h,\,w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i})=p_{i}\}
a set of layouts. Given an expression
\mathtt{E}
such that
\mathsf{ids}(\mathtt{E})\subseteq\{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{h}\}
and a register map
\rho
, there exists a value
v\in\mathsf{Val}
such that

\forall w\in W,\,\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}=v.

Proof.

We proceed by cases on the size of
W
. If
|W|=0
or
|W|=1
, the claim is trivial. Otherwise, we prove the following auxiliary claim:

\forall\mathtt{E}.\mathsf{ids}(\mathtt{E})\subseteq\{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{h}\}\Rightarrow\forall w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathsf{Layout}.\forall 1\leq i\leq h.w_{1}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i})=w_{2}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{id}}}_{i})\Rightarrow\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{1}}=\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w_{2}},

which can be proved by induction on the syntax of
\mathtt{E}
. The main claim follows from the IH on
W\setminus\{w\}
for some layout
w
, the application of the auxiliary claim to a pair of layouts
\overline{w}\in W\setminus\{w\}
and
w
, and the transitivity of equality.
∎

Remark 3
.

Let
p\in\mathsf{Addr}
be an address,
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}}\in{\mathsf{Arr}}
be an array, and
\tau
be a store. For
0\leq i<\mathsf{size}((){\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}})
, if
p=w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}})+i
, then

{(w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)[{p}\leftarrow{v}]}=w\mathrel{\diamond}({{\tau}[{(p,i)}\leftarrow{v}]}).

Proof.

The claim follows directly from unrolling the definitions.
∎

Remark 4
.

For every layout
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
, and pair of configurations
{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}
and
{(F^{\prime},m)}
such that
w\vdash_{\sigma}{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{*}{(F^{\prime},m)}
, it holds that
m=w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}
for some
\tau^{\prime}=_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on the length of the reduction. The base case follows from the reflexivity of
=_{\mathsf{FunId}}
. The inductive case follows from introspection of the rule that has been used for the last transition. The only non-trivial case occurs for the
rule, where the premise
p\in\underline{w}(\mathsf{ArrId}_{b})
guarantees the existence of a pair
({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},i)
such that
0\leq i<\mathsf{size}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}})
. The observation follows from
Remark

3
.
∎

Lemma 6
.

For every layout
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
, configuration
{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle,m)}
and non-empty stack
f:F
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and configuration
\phi
:

–

if
w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{n}{(F^{\prime},m^{\prime})}
, then:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle:f:F,m)}\to^{n}{(F^{\prime}:f:F,m^{\prime})}

–

if
w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{n}\mathsf{err}
, then:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle:f:F,m)}\to^{n}\mathsf{err}

–

if
w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{n}\mathsf{unsafe}
, then:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle:f:F,m)}\to^{n}\mathsf{unsafe}

Proof.

By induction on
n
. The base case is trivial. The inductive
case follows from the IH and by introspection of the rule that
has been used for showing the last transition. In particular, it suffices to
observe that every rule that can be applied to show

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(F^{\prime},m^{\prime})}\to{(F^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime})}

can also be applied to show

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(F^{\prime}:f:F,m^{\prime})}\to{(F^{\prime\prime}:f:F,m^{\prime})}.

∎

Lemma 7
.

For every layout
w\in\mathsf{Layout}
, configuration
{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}
and non-empty stack
\langle\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},b\rangle:F
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and configuration
\phi
:

–

if
\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})={v,\tau^{\prime}}
, then:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},b\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{*}\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho^{\prime}[{\mathit{ret}}\leftarrow{v}]},b\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}

–

if
\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})=\mathsf{err}
, then:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},b\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{*}\mathsf{err}

–

if
\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})=\mathsf{unsafe}
, then:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},b\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{*}\mathsf{unsafe}

–

if
\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})=\Omega
, then:

\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},b\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})=\Omega

Proof.

All cases follow directly from
Lemma

6
. The
first three cases are omitted, as the most interesting one is the
last.
Expanding the definition of
\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}(\cdot)
, we obtain that
\mathit{Eval}_{\sigma,w}({(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})=\Omega
is equivalent to stating that for every
n
,

\exists\chi.w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{n}\chi.

Our goal is to show that for every
n
:

\exists\chi^{\prime}.w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},b\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{n}\chi^{\prime}

Fix
n
. The claim follows by applying (†) to
n
and then using
Lemma

6
on the
n
-step reduction
w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to^{n}\chi
.
∎

Appendix for
Section

6

A.2.3
Buffered Memories

Buffered memories have already been defined in
Section

6
.
In this section we just give the formal definitions of their lookup
and flushing operations following
[
10
]
. The operation of
lookup a value from a buffered memory is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{(m)^{k}}(p)

\displaystyle\triangleq m(a),\bot

\displaystyle{({{[p\mapsto n]}\mathrel{:}{\mu}m})^{0}}(p)

\displaystyle\triangleq n,\bot

\displaystyle{({{[p\mapsto n]}\mathrel{:}{\mu}m})^{i+1}}(p)

\displaystyle\triangleq n^{\prime},\top

\displaystyle\text{if }{({{\mu}m})^{i}}(p)=n^{\prime},b

\displaystyle{({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto n]}\mathrel{:}{\mu}m})^{i}}(p)

\displaystyle\triangleq{({{\mu}m})^{i}}(p)

\displaystyle\text{if }p\neq p^{\prime}

and a function for flushing buffers:

\displaystyle\overline{m}

\displaystyle\triangleq m

\displaystyle\overline{{{[p\mapsto n]}\mathrel{:}{\mu}m}}

\displaystyle\triangleq{\overline{{{\mu}m}}[{p}\leftarrow{n}]}.

This function commits all the pending stores to the main memory. The
domain of a buffered memory is defined as follows:
\mathsf{dom}(\epsilon)=\emptyset
,

\mathsf{dom}({[p\mapsto v]}{\mu})=\{p\}\cup\mathsf{dom}({\mu})
.

Remark 5
.

if
{({{\mu}m})^{i}}(p)=v,\bot
, then
{({{\mu}m})^{0}}(p)=v,\bot
.

Proof.

the claim is:

\forall{{\mu}m}.\forall i.\forall v.{({{\mu}m})^{i}}(p)=v,\bot\to{({{\mu}m})^{0}}(p)=v,\bot

By induction on the length of the buffer.

-

Case

\epsilon
. The claim comes from the definition of lookup.

-

Case

{[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}{\mu}
. The IH says:

\forall i,v.{({{\mu}m})^{i}}(p)=v,\bot\to{({{\mu}m})^{i}}(p)={({{\mu}m})^{0}}(p)

and the claim is:

\forall i,v.{(({[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}\mu,m))^{i}}(p)=v,\bot\Rightarrow\\
{(({[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}\mu,m))^{i}}(p)={(({[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}\mu,m))^{0}}(p)

By cases on
i
.

-

Case

0
. The claim is:

\forall v.{({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}\mu m})^{0}}(p)=v,\bot\Rightarrow\\
{({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}\mu m})^{0}}(p)={({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}\mu m})^{0}}(p)

Observe that the conclusion is trivial.

-

Case

i+1
. The claim is:

\forall i.\forall v.{({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}\mu m})^{i+1}}(p)=v,\bot\Rightarrow\\
{({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}\mu m})^{i+1}}(p)={({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}\mu m})^{0}}(p)

Fix
i,v
, assume
{({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}\mu m})^{i+1}}(p)=v,\bot
, call this assumption (H). The claim becomes:

{({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}\mu m})^{0}}(p)={({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{v}]}\mathrel{:}\mu m})^{0}}(p)

Observe that it must be the case where
p^{\prime}\neq p
, otherwise from (H) and the definition of lookup, we obtain
\bot=\top
. With this assumption, from (H) we deduce
{({{\mu}m})^{i+1}}(p)=v,\bot
, and we can rewrite the claim as follows:

{({{\mu}m})^{i+1}}(p)={({{\mu}m})^{0}}(p)

The claim is a consequence of the IH.

∎

Remark 6
.

For every buffered memory
{{\mu}m}
, and every address
p
we have that

{({{\mu}m})^{0}}(p)=\overline{{{\mu}m}}(p)
.

Proof.

The proof goes by induction on
{\mu}
. If it is empty, then the claim is a
trivial consequence of the definition of lookup.
Otherwise, the claim is:

{({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto v]}:{\mu}m})^{0}}(p)=\overline{{{[p^{\prime}\mapsto v]}:{\mu}m}}(p),

that rewrites as follows:

{({{[p^{\prime}\mapsto v]}:{\mu}m})^{0}}(p)={\overline{{{\mu}m}}[{p^{\prime}}\leftarrow{v}]}(p).

If
p=p^{\prime}
, the claim is a consequence of the definition of lookup and memory update. Otherwise,
it is a consequence of the IH.
∎

Remark 7
.

For every buffered memory
{{\mu}(w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}

if
\mathsf{dom}({\mu})\subseteq w(\mathsf{ArrId})
,
then we have that:
\overline{{{\mu}(w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}}=w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}
for some
\tau^{\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the length of the buffer. If it is 0,
then the claim is trivial. Otherwise, it is a consequence of

Remark

3
.
∎

A.2.4
Omitted Proofs and Results

the memories

within a configuration that is reached during
the evaluation of a configuration whose memory is
w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau

is such that
m=w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}
for some
\tau^{\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau
.
This is justified by
Remarks

9
and
8
.

Proof of

{{(\langle{w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime}}(\llbracket\mathtt{F}\rrbracket_{\rho^{\prime},w}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},{w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime}},D,O)}}

and

{(\langle{w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime}}(\llbracket\mathtt{F}\rrbracket_{\rho^{\prime},w}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime},\mu{(w\mathrel{\diamond}\omega)},\bot)},

where
\rho_{0}^{\prime}={\rho_{0}[{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}\leftarrow{\llbracket\mathtt{E}_{1}\rrbracket{\rho^{\prime},w},\dots,{\mathtt{E}_{k}}{\rho^{\prime},w}}]}
. For this reason, the conclusion on the buffered memory is a consequence of the IH, and we can deduce

\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\langle{w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime}}(\llbracket\mathtt{F}\rrbracket_{\rho^{\prime},w}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime})

from the IH and the premises of the rule
, which guarantee
\llbracket\mathtt{F}\rrbracket_{\rho^{\prime},w}\in\underline{w}(\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{k}})
; this, in turn, has as consequence that there is
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}\in\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{k}}
such that
w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=\llbracket\mathtt{F}\rrbracket_{\rho^{\prime},w}
. Thus, by definition of
\cdot\mathrel{\diamond}\cdot
, we conclude that

{w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime}}(\llbracket\mathtt{F}\rrbracket_{\rho^{\prime},w})=\omega({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=\overline{\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}).

For this reason, it suffices to observe that
\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\overline{\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}))
holds by definition of system.

∎

Case

.

A.2.5
On the fencing transformation

Proof of
Theorem

3
.

This result is a direct consequence of
Lemmas

11
and
12

and of
Proposition

1
.
∎

We extend the non speculative semantics of our language
by defining the semantics of the

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fence}}}
instruction as follows:

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{fence}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle:F,m)}\to{(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle:F,m)}

In addition, in order to facilitate the proof of the
forthcoming results, we introduce the predicate
of
\eta
-
well-formedness
.
Given a system
\sigma=(\tau,\gamma,\xi)
, such predicate is defined
as follows:

\sigma\vdash\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{f}}}}\mathsf{wf}_{w,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\tau^{\prime})\lx@proof@logical@and\forall{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}\in\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{k}}.\exists\mathtt{C}.\tau^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=\eta(\mathtt{C}){\tau}\sim_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau^{\prime}

\sigma\vdash\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{f}}}}\mathsf{wf}_{w,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F)\lx@proof@logical@and\sigma\vdash\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{f}}}}\mathsf{wf}_{w,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(F)\exists\mathtt{C}^{\prime}.\mathtt{C}=\eta(\mathtt{C}^{\prime})

\sigma\vdash\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{f}}}}\mathsf{wf}_{w,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\varepsilon)\quad\sigma\vdash\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{f}}}}\mathsf{wf}_{w,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\mathsf{err})\quad\sigma\vdash\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{f}}}}\mathsf{wf}_{w,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\mathsf{unsafe})

\sigma\vdash\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{f}}}}\mathsf{wf}_{w,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}((F,{{\mu}(}w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}),b_{\!\mathit{ms}}))\lx@proof@logical@and\sigma\vdash\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{f}}}}\mathsf{wf}_{w,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\tau^{\prime})\sigma\vdash\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{f}}}}\mathsf{wf}_{w,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(F)\mathsf{dom}({\mu})\subseteq\underline{w}(\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{k}})

\sigma\vdash\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{f}}}}\mathsf{wf}_{w,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\phi:S)\sigma\vdash\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{f}}}}\mathsf{wf}_{w,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\phi)\quad\sigma\vdash\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{f}}}}\mathsf{wf}_{w,{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\epsilon)

n>1
: otherwise, if
n=1
, we would have
{x\ \mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\text{{*}}{\mathtt{E}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D}}=\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}})

, we deduce that there is a system

. This, in particular, would mean that

\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}})=\eta(\gamma^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}))
, so there is a command
\mathtt{C}\in\mathtt{Cmd}
such that

\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}})=\eta(\gamma^{\prime}(\mathtt{C}))
but, by induction on the syntax of the command,
we observe that this is not possible. However, from

12

\mathtt{C}
cannot contain system calls, so we can apply
Lemma

16
,

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}),\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle,\overline{{{\mu}(w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}})}\to^{n^{\prime}}\\
{{(\langle x\ \mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\text{{*}}{\mathtt{E}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F,\overline{{{\mu}^{\prime\prime}(w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime\prime})}})}}.

Finally, by assumption we know that the rule
has been applied
to show the transition in the speculative semantics. By introspection of that rule,
we conclude that its premises are also verified by the configuration

{{(\langle x\ \mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\text{{*}}{\mathtt{E}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F,\overline{{{\mu}^{\prime\prime}(w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime\prime})}})}};

this shows that
applies, i.e.

w\vdash_{\sigma}{(\langle x\ \mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\text{{*}}{\mathtt{E}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F,\overline{{{\mu}^{\prime\prime}(w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime\prime})}})}\to^{n^{\prime}}\mathsf{unsafe},

and this shows
\mathsf{CBU}(\sigma)
.
∎

Technical Observations

In order to show the analogous result but for the speculative semantics,
we define the judgment
\sigma\vdash\mathsf{wf}_{\sigma}(\cdot)
. For a system
\sigma=(\tau,\gamma,\xi)
,
it is defined as follows:

\sigma\vdash\mathsf{wf}_{\sigma}((\mathsf{err},\bot))\quad\sigma\vdash\mathsf{wf}_{\sigma}(\mathsf{unsafe})\quad\sigma\vdash\mathsf{wf}_{\sigma}(\epsilon)

\sigma\vdash\mathsf{wf}_{\sigma}((F,{{\mu}m},b_{\!\mathit{ms}}):S)\lx@proof@logical@and\sigma\vdash\mathsf{wf}_{\sigma}(S)\mathsf{dom}({\mu})\subseteq w(\mathsf{ArrId})m=w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau

Remark 8
.

, then we have
\sigma\vdash\mathsf{wf}_{\sigma}(S)
.

Proof.

The proof goes by induction on the number of steps.

-

Case
0. Trivial.

-

Case

n+1
. The claim is still a direct consequence of the IH, and in most of the cases the
proof is straightforward. The most interesting cases are those for stores and fences.
In the first case,the premise of the rule insures that the address where we write
is part of the memory of an array. In the second case, it is a consequence of

Remark

7
.

∎

Remark 9
.

{}{{}}{}

Remark 11
.

For every system
\sigma=(\tau,\gamma,\xi)
,
every store
\tau\sim_{\mathsf{Fun}}\tau^{\prime}
,
configuration

\phi={(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime},O,D)},

configuration
{(F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime},O,D)}

{(S,{(F^{\prime},O,D)})}
that are reachable in
n
step from
\phi
,
there are a pair of stacks
F_{\mathtt{k}},F_{\mathtt{u}}
such that

F^{\prime}=F_{\mathtt{k}}:F_{\mathtt{u}}
,
\mathtt{k}(F_{\mathtt{k}})
and
\mathtt{u}(F_{\mathtt{u}})
.

By induction on
n
. The base case is trivial, the inductive one comes by cases on the rule that is applied.
Most of these rules are relatively simple. We just show the most interesting cases:

. The target configuration carries a stack which is a suffix of the one in the source configuration,
so the conclusion is a trivial consequence of the IH.

Remark

8
, we deduce that
\tau^{\prime\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{FunId}}\tau
, this means that
{w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime}(p)}
, because from the premises of the rule, we know that
p\in\underline{w}(\mathsf{Fun}[b])
, which means that there is a function
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}\in\mathsf{Fun}[b]
such that
w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=p
. From this observation and the definition of
\cdot\mathrel{\diamond}\cdot
, we deduce that
w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime}(p)=\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})
. So, we go by cases on
b
. If it is
\mathtt{u}
, the body of the function is unprivileged, and this shows
w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime}(p)
, analogously, if it is
\mathtt{k}
, we observe that, this program is in
\mathtt{Cmd}
and does not have any
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ \cdot(\cdot)
instruction inside. This shows
\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}))
, as required.

-

Case

. Observe that this rule simply copies the stack from the source configuration
to the target configuration, and removes the executed command from the topmost frame; the conclusion is a consequence of the IH.

∎

In order to show a similar result, but for speculative execution,
we strengthen the predicate
\sigma\vdash\mathsf{wf}_{\sigma}(\cdot)
into
\sigma\vdash\mathsf{swf}_{w}(\cdot)
that is defined as follows:

\sigma\vdash\mathsf{swf}_{w}((\mathsf{err},\bot))\quad\sigma\vdash\mathsf{swf}_{w}(\mathsf{unsafe})\quad\sigma\vdash\mathsf{swf}_{w}(\epsilon)

Proof.

By induction on
n
. The base case is trivial, the inductive one comes by cases on the rule that is applied. Thanks to
Remark

8
,
we can just focus in showing the additional requirements on the stack. Most of these rules are relatively simple. We just show the most interesting cases:

-

Case

. The target configuration carries a frame-stack which is a suffix of the one in the source configuration, so the conclusion is a trivial consequence of the IH.

-

Case

. We apply the IH, and we rewrite the configuration reached after
n
steps as follows:

{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{E}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{E}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,b\rangle:F,{\mu^{\prime}w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau},b_{\!\mathit{ms}})\mathrel{:}S^{\prime\prime}}

we know by IH that it enjoys
\sigma\vdash\mathsf{swf}_{w}(\cdot)
, and
we observe that the target configuration looks like the following one:

(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{E}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{E}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho^{\prime},b\rangle:\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{k}\rangle:F,{\mu^{\prime}w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau},b_{\!\mathit{ms}})\mathrel{:}S^{\prime\prime}

for some
\rho_{0}
. From the premises of the rule, we know that
p\in\underline{w}(\mathsf{FunId}_{b})
, which means that there is a function
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}\in\mathsf{FunId}_{b}
such that
w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=p
. From this observation and the definition of
\cdot\mathrel{\diamond}\cdot
, we deduce that
w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime}(p)=\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})
. So, we go by cases on
b
. If it is
\mathtt{u}
, the body of the function is unprivileged, and this shows
w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime\prime}(p)
, analogously, if it is
\mathtt{k}
, we observe that, this program is in
\mathtt{Cmd}
and does not have any
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ \cdot(\cdot)
instruction inside. This shows
\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}(\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}))
, as required

-

Case

. We first apply the IH, which shows that the configuration
reached after
n
steps enjoys
\sigma\vdash\mathsf{swf}_{w}(\cdot)
, namely:

\sigma\vdash\mathsf{swf}_{w}((\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{E}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{E}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F,{\mu^{\prime}w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau},b_{\!\mathit{ms}})\mathrel{:}S^{\prime\prime})

form this observation, we deduce
that

\mathtt{u}({\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{E}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{E}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F})

must hold. The claim comes from introspection of the rule, and from the definition of

\gamma
.

∎

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.37688pt}{-7.31633pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -0.52 -10.12)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{}^{n}\,\chi,

and
\chi
has a mis-speculation flag (
\chi\neq\mathsf{unsafe}
) then the mis-speculation-flag of
\chi
must be
\bot
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction, and the base case is trivial. In the inductive case, we go by cases on the directive of the last transition:

-

Case

\mathsf{st}
. By introspection of this fragment of the semantics, we deduce that the number of stacks in the
n
-th and in the
n+1
-th target configurations is the same, so in particular the
n
-th target stack must have one entry only. For this reason we can apply the IH and deduce that the mis-speculation flag of the
n
-th target configuration
\bot
. Then we observe that for all these rules, the flag is always copied from the source configuration to the target one, so we conclude.

-

Case

\mathsf{ld}\,i,\mathsf{br}\,b
. For all the rules that match these directives, the claim hold for vacuity of the premise: the
n+1
-th target configuration stack has height greater than
1
. The only exception is
. In this case, the proof is analogous to the case of the same but with
\mathsf{st}
directive, which has already taken in account in the previous step.

-

Case

{\mathsf{bt}}
. We go by cases on the rule that has been applied. It must be one of
,
. We will just show the result for the ordinary configurations, the case for errors is analogous.

-

Case

. In this case, the claim follows directly from the definition of the rule.

-

Case

. Call
S_{n}
the
n
-th target configuration stack. Observe that its height cannot be neither
0
nor
1
. In the first case there would be no next transition, in the other case, we would get for the IH that its mis-speculation flag is unset, which is in contradiction with the assumption on the applied rule. Observe that if its height is greater than
2
, then the height of the
n+1
-th target stack is greater than
1
, so the claim holds for vacuity of the premise. Finally, if its height is exactly
2
, then it must be in the shape
\chi_{n}\mathrel{:}\chi_{n}^{\prime}
. From
Remark

14
, we deduce that there is a sequence of transitions from
\phi
to
\chi_{n}^{\prime}
whose length is
n^{\prime}\leq n
. By applying the IH on this sequence, we can show that
\chi_{n}^{\prime}
has mis-speculation flag unset. We conclude observing that the
n+1
-th target configuration stack is exactly
\chi_{n}^{\prime}
.

∎

(\langle\mathtt{C},{\rho[{x}\leftarrow{v}]},b\rangle\mathrel{:}F,{{\mu}m},b_{\!\mathit{ms}}\lor b^{\prime})

To do so, we can observe that, if we show that
{({{\mu}m})^{0}}(p)=v,\bot
,
then premises of the rule
are matched, and thus the proof is concluded.
This is a consequence of
Remark

5
.

-

Case

\mathsf{br}\,b
. Analogous to the case above.

-

Case

{\mathsf{bt}}
. If the rule which has been applied is
, the claim is a direct consequence of the IH. Otherwise, the rule applied is
. We go by cases on the height of
\chi:S
. If it is 0, we reached a contradiction, if its height is
1
, then the topmost configuration must have mis-speculation flag unset for
Remark

15
, ad this contradicts the assumption on the applied rule, which requires it to be set to
\top
. If the height is greater, we deduce that
\chi:S=\chi\mathrel{:}\chi^{\prime}\mathrel{:}S
and that the mis-speculation flag of
\chi^{\prime}
is
\bot
by introspection of the rule and by the main assumption of this claim. We apply
Remark

14
on the
n
-step reduction from
\phi
to
\chi\mathrel{:}\chi^{\prime}\mathrel{:}S
in order to show that there are
n^{\prime}\leq n
and a
n^{\prime}
-long reduction from
\phi
to
\chi^{\prime}\mathrel{:}S^{\prime}
. For the IH, there is a
n^{\prime\prime}\leq n^{\prime}
step reduction from
\phi
to
\chi^{\prime}
which employs only the directive
\mathsf{st}
. Which is our claim.

∎

where in particular
D^{\prime\prime}
does not contain
{\mathsf{bt}}
directives. We go by cases
on the directive
d
that is used in (†).

-

Case

d\neq{\mathsf{bt}}
. In these cases, the claim holds if we take
D^{\prime\prime}=D^{\prime}:d
,
O^{\prime\prime}=O^{\prime}:o
and
n^{\prime\prime}=n^{\prime}+1
.

-

Case

d={\mathsf{bt}}
. The applied rule can either be
, or
. We just take in exam the case of ordinary configurations:

-

Case

. In this case, we conclude that
S^{\prime}=\chi:S^{\prime\prime}
by introspection of the rule, we deduce that the mis-speculation flag of the topmost configuration of
S^{\prime}
is
\top
, so we apply
Remark

15
to deduce that
S^{\prime\prime}\neq\epsilon
(otherwise we could not have the flag set to
\top
). Thanks to this observation, we can use
Remark

14
to show that there is a sequence of transitions from
S
to
S^{\prime\prime}
whose length is
n^{\prime}\leq n
. The conclusion is a consequence of the application of the IH on this intermediate result. This concludes the sub-derivation.

-

Case

. In this case, we use
Lemma

13
to show that there is a sequence of transitions from
\phi
to
\phi
containing only the directive
\mathsf{st}
, which is stronger than the claim we need.

∎

For sake of simplicity, we define the relation
\precsim
as follows:

\varepsilon\precsim\varepsilon\quad\tau\precsim\tau^{\prime}\lx@proof@logical@and\tau\sim_{\mathsf{Id}_{\mathtt{u}}\cup\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{k}}}\tau^{\prime}\forall{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}\in\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{k}}.\tau^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=\eta(\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}))

\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F\precsim\langle\mathtt{C}^{\prime},\rho,\mathtt{k}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}}\rangle:F^{\prime}\lx@proof@logical@and F\precsim F^{\prime}\mathtt{C}^{\prime}=\eta(\mathtt{C})\quad\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F\precsim\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime}F\precsim F^{\prime}

\mathsf{err}\precsim\mathsf{err}\quad\mathsf{unsafe}\precsim\mathsf{unsafe}\quad{(F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\precsim{(F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\lx@proof@logical@and\tau\precsim\tau^{\prime}F\precsim F^{\prime}

Lemma 15
.

For every system
\sigma
, configurations

\phi

and

\chi
such that
\phi\precsim\chi
, if

\sigma\vdash_{w}\phi\to\phi^{\prime},

then

\eta(\sigma)\vdash_{w}\chi\to^{*}\chi^{\prime},

for some
\chi^{\prime}
such that
\phi^{\prime}\precsim\chi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The proof goes first by cases on
\phi
to refuse the cases
where it is
\mathsf{err}
or
\mathsf{unsafe}
. After that we can
assume that
\phi={(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,b\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}
,
and we go by cases on
b
.

-

Case

\mathtt{u}
.
We can assume that

\phi={(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}

and

\chi={(\langle\mathtt{C},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}.

with
F\precsim F^{\prime}
and
\tau\precsim\tau^{\prime}
. The proof goes by cases on the rule that has been applied to
\mathtt{C}

-

Case

.
The assumption is

\sigma\vdash_{w}{(\langle x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\mathtt{E}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{x}\leftarrow{\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}},

and by applying the same rule to
\chi
, we obtain

\eta(\sigma)\vdash_{w}{(\langle x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}\mathtt{E}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},{\rho[{x}\leftarrow{\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}}]},\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}}

and this shows the claim.

-

Case

. Analogous to the previous one.

-

Case

. Analogous to the case of assignments.

-

Case

. Analogous to the case of assignments.

-

Case

. Analogous to the case of assignments.

-

Case

. Analogous to the case of assignments.

-

Case

. The assumption is

\sigma\vdash_{w}{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F,{w\mathrel{\diamond}{\tau}[{p}\leftarrow{v}]})}},

where
v=\llbracket\mathtt{F}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}
and
p=\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}^{\mathsf{Addr}}
.
Observe that
p\in\underline{w}(\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}})
.
Which means that there is a pair
({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},i)
such that
w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}})+i=p
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}}\in\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}}
.
So, in particular, we have that
{w\mathrel{\diamond}{\tau}[{p}\leftarrow{v}]}={w\mathrel{\diamond}{{{\tau}[{({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},i)}\leftarrow{v}]}}}
because of
Remark

3
.
We also deduce that by applying the same rule to
\chi
, we obtain

\sigma\vdash_{w}{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}{{{\tau^{\prime}}[{({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},i)}\leftarrow{v}]}})}},

and to show the claim, we just need to observe
{{\tau}[{({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},i)}\leftarrow{v}]}\precsim{{\tau^{\prime}}[{({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}},i)}\leftarrow{v}]}
, which is a consequence of the assumption
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{a}}}\in\mathsf{ArrId}_{\mathtt{u}}
.

-

Case

. Analogous to the previous case

-

Case

.
The assumption is

\sigma\vdash_{w}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to\\
{(\langle w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau(\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle:{(\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)})},

where
\rho_{0}^{\prime}
is a shorthand for

\rho_{0}[x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\leftarrow\llbracket\mathtt{F}_{1}\rrbracket_{\rho,w},\dots,\llbracket\mathtt{F}_{1}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}]

and from the premise of the rule, we obtain that there is
{\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}}\in\mathsf{FunId}_{\mathtt{u}}
such that

\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}=w({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})
. Thus, from the definition of
w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau
,
we deduce that
w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau=\tau({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})=\tau^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{f}}})
for
the definition of the
\precsim
relation. Thanks to these observations,
we can show that the application of the same rule to
\chi
gives
rule to
\chi
, we obtain

\eta(\sigma)\vdash_{w}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{call}}}\ \mathtt{E}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})}\to\\
{{(\langle w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau(\llbracket\mathtt{E}\rrbracket_{\rho,w}),\rho_{0}^{\prime},\mathtt{u}\rangle:{(\langle\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F^{\prime},w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau^{\prime})})}},

and this shows the claim.

Case

.
The assumption is

\sigma\vdash_{w}{(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{syscall}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0547,0.4414,0.3477}{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathtt{F}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{F}_{k}){\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to\\

, we obtain

. The assumption is

\sigma\vdash_{w}{(\langle{\text{{*}}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{{:}{=}}}}}{\mathtt{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.2383,0.4336}{\mathtt{;\,}}}\mathtt{D},\rho,\mathtt{u}\rangle:F,w\mathrel{\diamond}\tau)}\to\mathsf{err},

.
This suffices to show

and to show the claim.
